=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
VA
June 6, 1941
Mr. Livesey:
With reference to the attached memo-
randum of conversation with Mr. Guy Emerson,
vice president of the Bankers Trust Company,
and Mr. Acheson's memorandum, I do not
believe that we could very well inform
Baker and Company through the Bankers Trust
Company that we did not approve of Baker and
Company's purchasing Japanese silver. So
long as our silver policy remains as it is
I suppose that if Baker and Company dces
not purchase the silver some other agent
will and will sell silver to the Treasury
under the Silver Purchase Act. I do think,
however, that we might express disapproval
of Baker and Company's borrowing on such
silver in order to pay the Japanese at the
dock. Thus, we could use the formula which
FE has used in similar cases to the effect
that in view of the policies of the Japanese
Government, particularly with reference to
Japanese treatment of American interests in
the Far East, the Department has indicated
when inquiry has been made of it that it
did not view with favor the extension of
credit to Japan.
GL
EA:LUTHRINGER:VCL U

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.97)
May 14, 1941
(Rector 2-8900)
Mr. Guy Emerson, V.P. of Bankers Trust Company/called
at suggestion of Mr. Bell in Treasury, with whom he had talked
about his problem.
(He had tried to talk to Mr. Acheson yesterday
(and this morning, when Mr. Acheson was busy.)
importing silver from Japan. It is a reputable firm andis
Government. The imports of silver from Japan amount to
several million dollars a year. Previously they have been
allowed fifteen days from the time the silver arrived at
San Francisco to pay for it. Recently the Japanese have
894.5
demanded payment at the dock, so that the Company has had
to borrow from Bankers Trust Co. and other banks. The bank
is anxious to know whether the e is any reason why this
1
financial transactlon should not go on. Mr. Emerson says
735
in reality they are financing the Japanese silver market.
He said the amount runs to five or ten million a year - that
there is not that much silver in Japan, and he does not
half
know where they get it. He said/jokingly they probably
stole it from the Chinese. The net result is that it puts
FLL2D
the Japanese in funds.
The firm uses quite a bit of the silver in manufac-
turing and sells the rest to the Treasury under the silver
P3
894.51
purchase act.
/LDP
He says if anyone sees any reason why it should
be looked into further they will be glad to cooperate. Mr.
Bell said he knew of no reason why they should not lend
Baker & Co. the money, but that inasmuch as things were

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
changing so rapidly, Mr. Bell thought it would be wise for
him to speak to Mr. Acheson before the next loan. I asked
when that would be. He said there was no particular date,
but that they might be called on at any time.
I asked him why they imported it from the Japanese,
and he said it was simply a competitive market; that there
was a very slight price advantage. He added that as a matter
of fact the last shipment was not up to the standard and
had to be resmelted.

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
The Depertment has been informed that the Japanese Government is en-
deavoring to borrow from the German Government peso funds to the equiva-
lent of 7,000,000 yen in order to effect part peyment for ox-hides bought
in Argentina. It is understood further that the Japanese ere offering
cocoanut oil and other goods in payment of the balance and they are anxious
to come to a speedy agreement since there are two Japenese ships waiting
to load the hides.
The British Embassy at Buenos Aires has reported that the Argentine
Central Bank has heard nothing of this proposal. The Embassy incidentally
does not think the Central Ben!k would permit the operation to be consum-
mated unless it were instructed to do so by the Argentine Government.
Please discuss the substance of this report in strict confidence
with appropriate Argentine authorities.

Lima, regarding activities of the Japanese, which I think will
be of interest to you.
I quote an extract from their letter below
" I knew you would be interested in Yokohana Specie
Bank credits. At the end of July 1 million dollars were
placed at the disposal of the Banco Italiano via the Chaa
National Bank and the Banco do Brazil of Rio de Janeiro to
894.51
finance purchases in Peru, Chile and Bolivia. By far the
largest sum was for use in Feru, mainly for cotton. ( I
have seen the details).
One e xplanati on of the possibility of a loeal varket
for yen is that nunerous Japare se have been for some weeks
going home from Feru and Chile. Only about 10% of them on
the last Jap ship which took over 200 fro Callao, applied
for re-entry permits. The will have sold up and have bought
their own curreney through their Consulates or Colony organ-
ization."

Sir:
Referring to the Embassy's tele gram no. 1057 of
September 30, 5 p.m. in regard to payment for Argentine
hides recently shipped to Japan, I have the honor to
enclose a copy of a memorandum on the subject prepared
PS/RH
by one of the assistant managers of the Buenos Aires
branch of the First National Bank of Boston (on September
30)
750

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
30) and furnished this morning by Mr. Lansing Wilcox,
head of the Argentine branches of that institution.
With respect to the purchase of yen by the Central
Bank, the memorandum confirms in general the information
received from another source, as reported in the Embassy's
telegram under reference. The memorandum states that it
is understood that after four or five weeks of difficulties
in obtaining shipment of 140,000 hides, owing to the
freezing of Japanese balances by our Government, "the
matter was finally cleared up when the Banco Central agreed
to purchase yen and those are supposed to have been taken
off their hands by the Japanese Government delivering
dollars to the Banco Central, which they held 1n Ecuador,
Perú and other South American countries".
Besides naming various parties in this country that
have been involved in supplying hides to Japan, the memo-
randum states that it is understood that "at present there
are in port about 45,000 hides in lighters awaiting a
Japanese boat which is supposed to arrive next month" and
that one of the parties mentioned "has seven lighters now
loading, presumably for Japanese account".
This information is being supplied to the British
Embassy.

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No....1......Despatch No.3205
MEMORANDUM
On making some inquiries around the market regarding
shipments of hides to Japan, I have been told that up to
the time that Germany declared war on Russia there as a
movement averaging about 200,000 hides per month taking
place. The business caused lots of hard feelings in the
hide trade owing to the fact that, while almost any price
was being paid for the hides, and, consequently, large pro-
fits obtainable, many of the local hide people had been re
quested not to participate in the business. Consequently,
those houses whioh do go in are supposed to have made a
tremendous amount of money at the expense of the others.
Among the houses mentioned as having participated are: -
Moos, Seifert, Kettelhohn and Villamil. As time went on
the frigorificos were all asked not to sell for this trade
and many of them agreed. They then turned to the Matadero
Market and continued the business, running prices up far in
excess of the comparative levels in the United States market.
There was also a lot of finished leather being exported
along towards the end.
As the business was being done in Dollars, the U. S. ac-
tion in freezing Japanese balances oreated a lot of trouble.
I was told that at the time the freezing went into effect
that Kettelhohn had 140,000 hides in lighters waiting for
letters of credit before loading on Japanese steamships. Moos
also is said to have had some. After four or five weeks I
understand that the matter was finally cleared up when the
Banco Central agreed to purchase Yens and those are supposed
to have been taken off their hands by the Japanese government
delivering Dollars to the Banco Central, which they held in
Eouador, Peru and other South American countries.

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
I understand that the other day some 208,000 hides
were shipped on a Japanese boat, although they were shipped
under Japanese names. They are supposed to have been for
account of Villamil, Kettelhohn, Seifert and Moos. The
names of the shippers, as appearing on the manifest, are: -
Y. TAKENCHI, who is said to ship for Seifert; I. IDA & CO.,
who is believed to ship for Kettelhohn and Moos; and S.
SHIMAZU and KANEVATZU RIO PLATENSE.
It is not known who
the last two represent. It
can be said, however, that
they have all come into existence within the past three
or four months, except for I. IDA & CO., who have been
shipping for about a year. I understand thet at present
there are in port about 45,000 hides in lighters awaiting
a Japanese boat which is supposed to arrive next month. I
und erstand that Moos has seven lighters now loading pre
sumably for Japanese account.
I am told that one local company wnich buys for Bri-
tish and American accounts was taking delivery of some
hides which they had purchased from a local frigorifico
recently, when they were asked by the frigorifico if they
would be good enough to give way to Moos and allow him to
receive hides which he had bought as th ere was urgent need
to load them on a Moore McCormack ship to the States. The
local house was agreeable and later found that Moos had
taken the hides but instead of loading them on the Moore
MoCormack ship sent them to Barracas and our informer now
believes they are the same hides on the lighter awaiting
a Japanese boat, as mentioned above.
On inquiries as to why after Russia got into the war
the Japanese should oontinue buying at exoessive prices
for Germany's acoount, I was told it was believed that
some

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.95)
-3 -
some wealthy Germans, fearing the aftermath of the war,
felt that they would be better off by putting their mo-
ney into commodities and shipping them to Japan while
they are able to obtain shipping facilities for disposal
after the war and in the meantime have comparative safe
ty. It also may be, of course, that the Germans antici-
pating viotory in Russie are piling up supplies as near
at hand as they can get them for use later.

Sir:
With reference to the Imbassy's despatch no. 3205
of Ootober 1 last, conoerning payment for Argentine hides
recently shipped to Japan, I have the honor to report that
Dr. gardo Grumbach, head of the For eign Exohange Depart-
ment in the Central Bank, supplied further information on
the subject in a conversation which he had yesterday with

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 
of the British Embassy. Dr. Grumbach is reported to
have stated that while it was true that the Central Bank
had furniahed the pesos for payment of the merohandise in
question, most of these funds came from the compensation
aocount, and were furnished in accordance with the provi-
sions of the compensation agreement, If any dollars held
by the Japanese in other parts of South Amerioa had been
received by the Central Bank in payment of these pesos,
according to Dr. Grumbach, the amount was negligible.
With respect to the hides which apparently are to be
shipped to Japan in the near future, as reported in the
Embassy's despatch under reference, Dr. Grumbach said that
the Central Bank had not as yet been approached in the mat-
ter.

Sir:
Referring to the Embassy's telegrams nos. 1l50 of
October 17, 7 p.m. and 1159 of 0ctober 18, 1 p.m., in
regard to the arrival here aboard the Toa Maru of 73
cases of gold, which had apparently been shipped
from the Yokohama Specie Bank to the Central Bank of
Argentina, I have the honor to state that it has now
NOV 2 4 1941
been
FILED
Carbon copios
destmRm

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
been reported on good authority that the gold in question
was officially valued, as low-grade metal, at only
7,357,000 pes0s (approximately $1,750,000 U.S.currency
at the prevailing free-market rate of exchange), although
the real value may be nearer 10,000,000 pesos (approximately
$2,375,000 U.S.currency).
As was reported in the former of the telegrams under
reference, however, the vessel, according to an official
of the Central Bank, sent a radio message to that institu-
tion prior to arrival stating that it had on board gold worth
$5,000,000 U.S.currency but the bank thought the figure
might be in the neighborhood of 10,000,000 U.S.currency.
Later, however, the Embassy was informed (as was reported
in the Embassy's telegram no. 1159 of October 18) by a
well informed commercial source (who is the manager of a
warehouse) that the weight of the gold on the manifest
was 2,676 kilograms which would mean a value of approxi-
mately $3,000,000 U.S.currency if pure gold.
There was a report that this shipment of gold was
consigned to the Central Bank by order of and for the
account of the Banco de la Provincia de Buenos Aires as
security for a loan of 10,000,000 pesos to cover shipments
of products to Japan. Information received today, however,
indicates that the Central Bank is dealing directly with
the Yokohama Specie Bank and that this shipment of gold
is for purposes of shipments of Argentine products to
Japan on the Toa Maru, which is scheduled to leave here
October 24.
With reference to the Embassy's despatches nos. 3205
of October 1, 1941, and 3209 of October 3, 1941, concerining
recent

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
cent shipments of hides to Japan and difficulties in
arranging for their payment, there is enclosed a transla-
tion of a copy of what is said to be the complete bill of
lading for the return voyage of the Toa Maru as prepared
from official documents.
It is reported that after about 50 tons of quebracho
extract supplied by the quebracho trust (La Forestal
Argentina) had been loaded, the trust learned of a
quantity of the article awaiting shipment in the same
vessel furnished by independent producers in violation
of the trust's contract with the Japanese, whereupon,
by orders from London, it cancelled the contract.
Respectfully yours
Norman Armour

Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's despatch no. 3354
of October 22 last reporting detai ls of the cargo of the
Japanese steamship, Toa Maru, which sailed from Buenos
Aires for Japan on October 25, I have the honor to enclose
1/
a translation of the final manifest of the vessel as
published here on October 28. It will be noted that there
are
NEN

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2-
are several additions and changes in this list as compared
with the one enclosed in the Embassy's despatch aforemen-
tioned.
There were finally shipped 802,000 kilograms (802 metric
tons) of quebracho extract, supplied by the quebracho trust.
Although as reported in the Embassy's despatch of October 22,
the trust received orders from London to stop the loadings
(in view of independent production having been bought by
the Japanese contrary to the trust contract), these instruc-
tions are reported to have been changed when an agreement
was reached with the Japanese that there would not be loaded
any quebracho supplied by independent producers.
It is also to be mentioned that the 7 tons of wolfram-
ite and 13 tons of scheelite which had been loaded and
which were included in the former list, were removed from
the vessel following representations made by Mr. Thomas J.
Williams of the Metals Reserve Company.
The number of hides finally loaded and shipped on
this vessel, according to the published manifest, was
44,736.

suBJEcT: Proposed Japanese Transactions with the Bank of Brazil
PARTiciPAnTs: Mr. Walter J. Donnelly, Commercial Attaché at Rio;
Mr. Ware Adams, RA.
OFFICE OF THE ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
COPIES TO:
AUg 104
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
894.51/762
ene
1-1408
Mr. Donnelly telephoned from the Embassy to say that
the Japanese had filed thirty-two applications with the
Bank of Brazil to open credits in the South American
countries, principally Chile, Argentina and Bolivia, for
the purchase of strategic materials. The applications
PS/PLS
total1ed $1,400,000.
The Director of Exchange of the Bank of Brazil informed
Mr. Donnelly immediately upon receipt of the applications.
Mr. Donnelly immediately told the Director that the opening
of these credits would run counter to our policies in the
matter of credits as well as strategic materials, and
ON
FILED

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.94)
-2-
expressed the strong hope on behelf of the Embassy that
the Bank of Brazil would decline to comply with the
Japanese requests in continuation of the much appreciated
cooperation that the Bank of Brazil has been giving our
Government right along.
The Director of Exchange told Mr. Donnelly that he
did not want to do anything that could not be approved
under our freezing reguletions and that he would so inform
the Japanese. It was evident, however, that the position
of the Director of Exchange vis-a-vis the Japanese would
be elearly strengthened if he could be given a legal ob-
jection to opening the credits instead of having to take
this position merely as a matter of policy on the part of
the Bank of Brazil.
7
1a
RA: Adamm: GLW

Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Morgenthau:
Your letter of December 19th, received this morning
and in reply beg to say that I purchased yesterday through the
Citizens Bank and Trust Company a Bond of $1o00.00 maturity
value, and will probably purchase more this month.
I was thinking of writing to you when I came up to
the bank this morning in regard to 5,O00.00 Bonds of the Imperial
Japanese Government. I bought these Bonds a number of years ago
when the issue was floated by the National City Bank. Now that we
are at War with Japan, and all Japanese funds in the United States
are seized, I was wondering it it was possible for our Government
to collect this money from the Japanese funds seized in this Country
and save me from what appears to be a total loss.
Very truly yours,
Mailey Clwer

894.51

Tokyo's despatch no. 5987, November 19, 1941
encloses a copy of the Commercial Attaché's re-
port covering the period from November 3 - 17,
1941.
The first six pages review the address to
the House of Representatives of the Minister of
Finance, in which the Minister paints an
unusualiy optimistic picture of Japen's financial
and economic condition, stating that the finences
of Japan remain very sound; thet, since the
beginning of the Chine Affair, appropriations
for emergency military expenditures had reached
yen 26 billion:and that these allotments together
with those made under general account aggregated
yen 48 billion, a sum far exceeding Government
expenditures from the first year of Meiji to
1936. The Finance Minister stated that Japan hed
invested over yen 23 billion in Manchuria end
China since the outbreak of the Chine Incident,
these investments having been made only in those
industrial branches considered most essential
and urgent under present emergency conditions;
that with regard to the supply of goods for
civilian consumption there was much to be desired:
and that more extensive and stringent control
measures would be of necessity employed to con-
serve importent materials and supplies.
The Commercial Atteché reported that the
Government

-2
Government was introducing a bill into the Diet
providing for a tax increase, principelly on
luxuries, estimated st yen 630 million annually.
He further reports that it was evident that bond
absorption was becoming more and more uncertain.
It is learned from the enclosures to the
report that at the end of October, 194l, the
funded national debt of Japan stood at nearly
35 b1llion yen, an inorease of nenly 25
billter yen an increase of nearly 9 billion
yen in one year. A National Treasury Statement,
as of the end of September, 194l, showed annual
revenue (not including public loans and
surpluses from previous accounts) amounting
to Yen 1,263,604,000, an increase of Yen
323,707,000 over the previous year. Thus,
in i94l, national debt stood in a ratio of
27.7 to public revenue, as compared with
27.6 per cent in 1940. It wouid be of interest
to know the ratio between national debt and
public revenue on the one hand to national
wealth and national income on the other hand,
but reliable estimates in regard to national
wealth and national income are not available.
These figures, however, have no real
significence so far as relates to measuring
Japan's future war capacity.

IMPERIAL ORDINANCE NO 891
TEMPORARY MAIL CONTROL ORDINANCE -
(Translated by the Okuyama Service)
from Official Gazette of October 4 1941
Article 1.
At wartime (including the case of incidents in
the nature of war, herein as well as hereinafter).
the Minister of Communications may, if he deems it
necessary for protection of national defense interest,
prohibit or restrict the dispatch of mails in accord-
ance with the stipulation of an order.
Article 2.
At wartime, the Minister of Communications may,
if he aeems it necessary for protection of national
defense interest, have the competent officials inspect
the mail matters other than those mentioned hereunder.
1. Mails disoatched from the Japanese government
officer (including army and navy units and
schools, herein as well as hereinafter) with
a fixed indication thereon, and mails address-
ed to the Japanese government offices.
2. Sealed letters and letter-cards for domestie
crdinary mails (not including those which are
unáer suspicion to contain communioation or
matters to be delivered to or from person
living outside of the place of enforcement of
this Ordinance).
Artiele

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
Article 3.
The Minister of Communicati no may, in accordance
with the tipulatione of an ordr euse the ender or
receiver of mails to make certifi tlons indications
or other necessary arrangements wrich are necessary
for the control of mails under this rdinance.
Article 4.
The Minister of Communications may suspend the
delivery of such mails which are detrimental or con-
sidered detrimental to the national defense interest,
or such mails which have indistinct centents, out of
those mails which have been subjected to the inspec-
tion。
Article 5.
A person who has acted in contravention of proh1-
bition or restriction in accordance with the provisions
of Article 1 shall be liable to a fine not exceeding
¥1,ooo. The same shall apply to a person who has acted
against an order issued on the basis of Article 3, or
who has made a false stetement.
Article 6.
A person who 1s angaged or has been engaged in the
business of inepesting mails under the present Ordi-
nance, if he divulges the privacy of correspondence
he has learned through the performance of his duty,
shall be liable to a penal servitude not exceeding two
years or to a fine not exceeding ¥2,000.

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
An attempt on the foregoing offenses shall also
be punished
Article 7.
The Minister of Communications as mentioned in
this Ordinance shall be the Gov rnor General of Chosen
in case of Chosen the Governor-General of Taiwan in
case of Taiwan, Envoy Extraordinary and Ambassador
Plenipotentiary to Manchoukuo in case of Kwantung
Leased Territory, the Governor of Saghalien in case
of Saghalien and the Governor of South Seas in case
of the South Sea Islands.
Supplementary Rule
This Ordinance shall be enforced as from the day
of its promulgation.

As you know, the Superintendent of Banks took possession of
the New Tork Agency of Yokohama Specie Bank, Ltd., for the
purpose of liquidation, on December 8, 194l. Thereafter
the Superintendent vacated the premises occupied by the
bank at 120 Broadway, as well as the premises occupied by
the Japanese Financial Commission in the same building. At
that time the furniture in the Commission's office was re-
moved and has since been kept in storage by the Superintendent.
The moving charges amount to $180.00 and the storage charges
are contimuing to accrue.

furniture while it may still have sufficient value to meet
the aforeaid charges.
We feel that it will simplify matters in this direction 1f
you will write us athorizing the Superintendent to make such
sale.
We would appreciate your immediate response containing such
authorization.

June24, 1942 4
There is given below the text of the speeoh made by
Finance Minister Okinobu Kaya to the Japanese Diet, May 27,
1942.
(Note: The following text has been copied from the
F.C.C. teletype text received by the Foreign Broadcast
Monitoring Service in Washington from its receiving station
in Portland, Oregon, May 29, 1942, 1:05 p.m.)
I consider it a great honor to have this opportunity
894.
to explain to you the contents of the supplementary budget
for the fiscal year 1942 and the present financial and
economic conditions of Japan. In the first place I should
like to say a few words on the contents of the supplementary
5
budget for 1942. At this time the amount of additional
51/78
expenditure presented before the Diet for the construction
of ships of general class 1s 12,850,000 yen. The total
expenditures presented in these bills are needed for the
85
expansion and provision made in regard to (facilities for
seamen's training) and for materials needed for the
standard shipbuilding program which has for its purpose
the firm establishment of a plan to build standardized type
ships, the measures of which are urgent under the present
ciroumstances. The general outline of the entire amount
of fiscal budget since the outbreak of the Greater East
Asia War(?)our finances have seen a sudden increase and
have expanded and have in truth expanded to ten times those
at the time of the China incident. However despite the
fact that we have continued to carry out the great polioy
you have cooperated without regrets and have responded
well to the (purchase of bonds), thus showing the un-
swervingly strong finance of the Greater East Asia behind
the home front. Despite the difficult conditions facing
the people today, the House of Representatives that has
received the approval of the people is now carrying out
oareful study as to management, laws and regulations and
improvements.
JUN251942
Therefore, the prioes of commodities in general and
FILED
of many other things have not been disturbed in the least
and Japan's finance system is truly seoure. With respect
to the war front, transportation ciroles, economic
ciroles and various other fields, the heavy responsibility
of sending (supplies) to the (fronts) is being fulfilled
PS/MM
without

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
without any fear for the unprecedented Great War, and the
Government expects to build up the living conditions of
the people behind the home front in the future. It is
encouraging to know that an immovable and promising
economic structure is evidenced despite the fact that the
nation has plunged into a Great War to overcome the
reliance on American and Britain that continued for the
past several decades. The brilliant war results achieved
by the Imperial forces have been due to the united coopera-
tion of the people and this is truly beyond the scope of
appreciatfon. The foundations of the finandial and economic
policy of our country reflect on the Greater East Asia War
and the firm establishment of the Greater East Asia Co-
Prosperity Sphere and the above is based on the spirit
of a new economic foundation concentrating on progress.
In order to promote this our country will not play a lone
role but all the countries within the Greater East Asia
Co-Prosperity Sphere must fully extend their cooperation.
The firm establishment of the Greater East Asia economic
power must come and on this objective all the countries
within the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere must
have as their sole mission the plan to spread throughout
the eight directions. In short this does not only mean that
profits will be divided among the various races in all the
areas of the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere but
ahall serve as its basis.
Furthermore at the same time it shall always in all
the areas (). In connection with the attainment of this
natural objective since our country is to become the main
core and occupy the leading position it is only natural
that our country should expend its fullest efforts. Each
area within the Co-Prosperity Sphere must realize together
its mutual ob jectives which could be attained by fulfilling
1ts share in the fields of finance and economy. By means
of hard work and earnest endeavor and the support of the
Government by the people, the greater establishment is
effected and opens a bright future of co-prosperity within
the sphere of tens of thousands of peoples. By conforming
to the fundamental structure the various financial and
economic facilities within the various areas of the so-
prosperity sphere...should be accomplished. By taking
advantage of the proper time, all the problems in the various
occupied areas in the southern region shall be dealt with
and controlled as one, and in order to firmly establish and
develop the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere as well
as the completion of the Greater East Asia War, each and
every area must exert its fullest cooperation. I have a
strong conviction that this can be realized. The fundamental
pol1cy

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
policy to be taken by us has only one definite path with
the purpose of establishing a great future policy of co-
prosperity within the sphere of increasing the rights of
our country within the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity
Sphere and the managing the production and interchange of
materials among the various areas within the sphere. There-
fore, the financial policy of the Greater East Asia shall
be carried out parallel with the foregoing policy. In
the execution of this policy it is only natural for us to
adopt a gold standard with Japan as its core in the glorious
unification of a new world and by meeting the supply and
demand within the sphere. For this reason t he various areas
in the co-prosperity sphere shall not only be united
together but be controlled in Tokyo. Hence it shall serve
as the central passageway in the Gratb East Asia and the
policy that Tokyo must be regarded as the center has been
already made clear. In so far as "Manchukud" and Nanking are
concerned such a policy is already being carried out.
Recently Thailand joined in this cooperative unit. With
this policy in mind and through the alliance concluded
between Thailand and Japan, the past control of gold and
American and British silver will be wiped out. In their
places the Japanese yen will be used.Furthermore this
method shall also apply to the close alliance among the
various areas within and without the sphere of the Orient.
For this reason Thailand realized the necessity of carrying
out transactions by joining with the banking system of Japan.
The Bank of Japan has undergone a reorganization so that
it may serve as the central structure of the Greater East
Asia financial sphere, besides fulfilling the role of
establishing a structure for the defense of the country, the
first step toward advancement is shown by the settlement of
trade accounts with Thailand. Furthermore, recently the
exchange rate between Japan and Thailand has been changed
and fixed on a par basis. The fixing of the exchange ra te
at par will reasonably compell the flow of money and materials
from the standpoint of achieving the universal objective,
which is the advancement of the co-prosperity sphere and
the future prosecution of the Greater East Asia War. The
present exchange, based upon the international principle of
laissez-faire, were established in accordance with capacity
of settling accounts.
The Greatetr East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere shall be
established in accordance with the living conditions of the
various areas and it shall lay great emphasis on the completion
of cooperation based upon an entirely new structure. At
this time I wish to express my sincere confidence that the
establishment

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
establishment will be smoothly carried out. That the Thai
Government is today cooperating with us to a considerable
extent in the phase of financial relations toward the ad-
vancement of the firm establishment of the Greater East
Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere in turn means its own progress
and (prosperity). The exchange rates in the Greater East
Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere shall be accomplished in the
manner (I have explained to you) but at the same time the
main conception of Greater East Asia prosperity will see a
new birth. London and New York have been able to occupy the
heart of international finance having deeply rooted them-
selves only in their financial power and the power to act
as clearing houses based on the international principle of
laissez-faire and capitaliam. However the heart to be
occupied by our country in Greater East Asia finance means
that Japan will exert every effort universally and organically,
for the completion of the Greater East Asia War and the
advancement and firm establishment of the Greater East Asia
Co*Prosperity Sphere with our nation as the main guiding
power. In the event there is miscarriage in the policy
in any of the various areas, the Government will exercise
control and make other arrangements for these areas in
accordance with the above fundamental financial structure.
In regard to currency circulated in the various occupied
areas of the southern regions, our military notes have mixed
and joined with the circulating currency in those areas
and are effecting a very smooth circulation. Furthermore
in the latter part of March the Southern Development Bank
was established and has added greatly to the resumption
of (operation of the various) banks in those areas. Besides
pushing forward in the resumption of operation of other
facilities the general situation of money circulation has
seen steady adjustments, thus bringing about the resumption
of the active operation of various machineries, the deveop-
ment of natural resources, and the accumulation of materials.
It is needless for me to point out that the role played by
both 'Manchukud' and Nanking in the Greater East Asia Co-
Prosperity Sphere is absolutely important to us. The
development of natural resources vital to national defense
and the economic establishment in the various fields in
"Manchukud" and Nanking have been actively and continuously
carried out and have seen steady progress with the financial
and material aid given by Japan. Furthermore ever since
March 31, the Hanking Government has abolished the relation-
ship of equal value between the old fah pei and the
Central Reserve Bank note. As a result the Central Reserve
Bank note severed its relationship with the declining old
legal tender. Hence the Central Reserve Bank note has
attained an independent value and has prepared a way for
the stabilization of currency which will become the basic
financial

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
financial power of Central China. This is truly an appropriate
measure in these times which prepares for the birth of a
new (circulation of currency in Central China and therefore
the economic significance can be considered extremely
important. At this moment the measures taken by the Govern-
ments can bring about satisfactory results but as for the
Japanese Government also it has been its policy to extend
support to the policies of the Nanking Government by
strengthening mutual cooperation and it intends to continue
further guidance and cooperation.
As for the fundamental domestic policy in war t ime the
Government will furnish the necessary funds but it shall
not be met by the issuance of new money. However there is
no need for me to mention the emphasis laid by the Govern-
ment on the encouragement of national savings. Ever since
the outbreak of the Greater East Asia War, savings have seen
a marked increase, the increase during the four months
between December 194l and March 1942 has reached a figure
of 6,830,000,000 yen.Gomparing the figures of the above
period with the previous year, there is a sudden increase
of 2,360,000,000 yen which is nearly 70 per cent. This is
in truth the expression of the people's earnest efforts
in regard to patriotic savings and displays the burning
enthusiasm of the people for the completion of the Greater
East Asia War. I have no way to express my heartfelt
appreciation. This is the evidence of the people's
patriotic contributions which is seen in the records of
savings. The tremendous savings increase during 1941
exceeded 16 billion yen and I can say generally speaking
we have attained a good start. From the beginning of the
China Incident to March 1942, the actual figure of the
increase in the people's savings reached 50 billion yen.
As a result of these savings, the Government has ful-
filled the needs of the tremendous war expenditures and the
expansion of productive power. The amount of the increased
savings this year is 23 billion yen, which is an increase of
7 billion yen compared to last year. In order to attain
this goal I feel that it will require greater efforts but
as long as the national savings increase at the rate shown
at the beginning of this year, war expenditures can be
fully covered and there will not be the slightest worry
of severe inflation. I earnestly desire that the people
with further determination cooperate for the realization
of our objective.
To carry out the domestic financial policy through an
important organ, it is essential that the collected fund be
utilized to the fullest extent and it is important in the
attainment

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
attainment of the objective of the war that the fund be
supplied wherever needed and that the flow of capital in
unimportant directions be totally blocked. (In order to
coordinate) the general sea transportation and capital on
land and at the same time to cover the shortage of capital,
the Government has created the monetary control organiza-
tions which are essential to the strengthening of the
domestic combined structure. The various monetary control
associations in the country and other organizations have
been joined and the operations of these have already started.
The Government holds high expectation in regard to their
future. In addition the wartime finance banks were established
in April and the Government has established methods to
effect special loans under wartime conditions. Through
these banks the Government expects to effect loans for ship-
building and for facilities and machinery which are im-
portant under present conditions.
Now turning to our enemy. America has turned to war
industries and other important industries for the first
time since having been discouraged by the serious defeats
of the Allied forces. Because of the defeats suffered
at the outset and the tremendous expansion of the special
war expenditures, President Roosevelt foresaw the inevitable
severe inflation and surprised by the possible outcome
presented a special message before Congress on April 27
at which time he (proposed the reduction of the living
expenses of the people.....and the increase of savings).
Such proposals presented and announced by President Roosevelt
as wartime economic policy can be taken as only natural.
This has created great disturbance and fear in America:
Compared to the above I must say that the confusion
in the United States makes us laugh. (Although they are
our enemies I feel sorry for them?. We should pledge
ourselves that such a thing will (not) come to us. By
successfully fighting through the Greater East Asia War
and by attaining complete victory andcountry as well as to
firmly establish, maintain and develop the Greater East
Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere, the earnest and absolute efforts
on the part of the people are yet to be given in field
of Greater East Asia economy. Greater emphasis must be
laid on the construction of the various areas and its
policies. In order to carry out these policies more firmly
and excellently, as well as to extend the resolute willing-
ness, which means undivided cooperation, are necessary(). I
have

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.94)
-7-
have a firm belief in the successful conclusion of the
war (with the people doing their duty through patriotic
savings and complete unity for they are filled with true
patriotism. In conclusion, I wish to express my earnest
desire that prompt approval be given to the bilis presented
by the Government.

Tokyo's despatch No. 60l4, dated
February 16, 1942 transm1ts a report
from the Commercial Attaché on the
National Budget for Japan for the year
1942-43.
The national budget rose from
22 million Yen in the preceding year
to 27 billion Yen in the current year,
chiefly because of the increase in the
extraordinary military expenses for
this year, 18 bill1on Yen as compared
with 12 billion Yen last year. The
war appropriation for this year brings
the grand total appropriated so far
for war purposes since July, 1937, to
47 billion Yen. The budget is to be
financed by taxes (29%) and by bonds
(66%). With reference to the disposal
of bond issues in February it is stated
that in 194l 83.9% were absorbed. This
percentage was higher than in 1940.
The Commercial Attaché quotes the
Minister of Finance as estimating that
the national income of Japan during
the current year will reach 45 billion
Yen, Or this amount the Minister
announced that 15 billion Yen would be
appropriated

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
appropriated for living expenses of the
people, about 23 billion for public
financing and about 6 billion Yen for
industrial investment. The Commercial
Attaché points out that while the
Russo-Japanese War cost Japan one and
one-half billion Yen,Japan was forced
to borrow 800 million Yen of this sum
from abroad; but that in the present
conflict Japan has already appropriated
47 billion Yen and has not borrowed one
cent. Mr. Williams states that in
addition Japan has redeemed 96 million
1937.
Yen of foreign loansice

incomes of Japsnese individuals end corporations resid-

estimates for nationel income aurin: tne next fiscal

tre people, about Yen 25 billion for public financing

exoansion funds.

that

that while the Russo-Japanese war cost Japan only

Finr?
The Foreign Minister's speech in connection with
the ordinary budget was as follows:
"Adjustment of the present budget was achieved on
the basis of a previous decision by the Cabinet of a
budget compilation policy. In view of present conditions,
such matters as commodities, capital, etc., and mobili-
zation plans were taken into consideration. From the
standpoint of the principles of caring for important
items and paring expenses to the bone, and as far as
possible avoiding increased and new items of expenditure,
the Cabinet decided that the items decided upon should
be appropriated for as far as the scope of financial
circumstances permits. On the other hand, the Cabinet
recommended that as far as possible economies should be
effected in approved items when there is a change in
conditions.
"Since September (1942) each Ministry's requests
have been examined in detail in adjusting the general
accounts, and after continued negotiations between the
various Ministries and the Ministry of Finance, I am
truly happy to say that the various Ministries have
finally cooperated in seeing eye to eye with Cabinet
decisions."

The largest increase in departmental ex-
penditures is found in the allotment to the Ministry
of Finance. This is indicative of the rapidly grow-
ing national debt and the mounting volume of funds
necessary for interest charges. Larger grants to
the Department of Commerce & Industry undoubtedly re-
present wider govemment participation in industrial
production of munitions and other war supplies, as
well as provisions for obtaining greater quantities
of raw materials from occupied areas. Expansion in
other appropriations reflect greater emphasis on in-
creased agricultural output, extension of industrial
facilities, improvement of communications and trans-
portation, and particularly the necessity of larger
expenditures in occupied areas.
REVENUE
Receipts against the basic General Accounts
budget of Yen 9,995,000,000 are estimated as fo 1lows:-
Taxes and other recurring revenues Yen 6,453,000,000;
bond 1ssues Yen 1,880,000,000, and other extraordinary
receipts Yen 1,662,000,000. Receipts from new and in-
creased taxes are estimated at Yen 1,143,000,000, and
other monopoly goods at Yen 450,000,ó00 - a total of
Yen 1,593,000,000. The estimated gain 1n tax revenue
from these sources for 1942-43 over 1941-42 was
Yen 1,950,000,000.
One statement on the budget indicates a
bond issue for 1943-44, exclusive of the Extra-
ordinary Military Expenditures Account, of
Yen 3,260,000,000 for the General Account, and Yen
1,050,000,000 for the supplementary budget, a total
of Yen 4,310,000,000 and representing a sharp gain of
Yen 1,953,000,000 over the estimate for 1942-43.
Reports from Tokyo are to the effect that
the Minister of Finance informed the Diet that the
total bond issue for the calendar year of 1942 reached
Yen 13,321,000,000, an increase of Yen 4,539,000,000
over l94l. The Minister reportedly further stated that
the rate of absorption rose sharply to 96.2% as against
83.9% for 1941 and 78.5% for 1940. These figures indi-
cate a very substantial improvement in the general
participation of the Japanese people and financial in-
stitutions in the nation's war efforts.
The total authorized bond issue for the
1942-43 f1scal year was Yen 16,487,000,000, Yen
14,129,803,000 for the Special Military Appropriation,
and Yen 2,357,000,000 for General Accounts. This total
gives an average monthly issue of Yen 1,600,000,000,
while the actual issue of Yen 13,321,000,000 represented
a monthly issue of only Yen 1,1l0,000,000 for the
calendar year.
EXTRAORDINARY

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)
EXTRAORDINARY MILITARY BUDGET
Information has just come to hand to the
effect that the Japanese Government has submitted to
the 8lst session of the Diet the Extraordinary
Military budget for 1943-44 amounting to the huge
sum of Yen 27,000,000,000. This compares with the
Yen 18,000,000,000 appropriation for 1942-43 and
brings the total expenditures authorized under this
allotment since July, 1937, to Yen 74,128,567,000.
(For detailed appropriations for the years 1937 to
1942 see Despatch No. 6014 from the Tokyo Embassy,
dated February 16, 1942, subject: "National Budget
for Japan - 1942-43 Fiscal Year", page 4.)
Revenue from bonds is estimated at
Yen 17,163,000,000; from 1oans Yen 3,300,000,000; from
funds to be transferred from tax accounts
Yen 4,913,000,000 (1ncluding Yen 4,336,000,000 from
the General Accounts, Yen 577,000,000 from the
Special Accounts of communications enterprises, rail-
ways and from the Governments of Chosen, Taiwan,
Kwantung and Karafuto); Yen 18,000,000 from an un-
designated special account, and Yen 1,600,000,000
from miscellaneous accounts, or a total of
Yon 26,994,000,000. The bond 1ssue of Yen 17,163,000,000
exceeds that for the previous year by Yen 3,033,197,000,
while the total bond issue for the combined budgets
(general and military) at Yen 21,473,000,000, is
greater by Yen 4,986,000,000 than for 1942-43. (Note:-
Japanese sources give this excess as Yen 5,240,000,000,
a difference of Yen 254,000,000, which may be accounted
for by a possible bond authorization during the 80th
session of the Diet of which I have no record.)
From July, 1937 to March 31, 1942 the
authorized bond issue for war expenditures reached
Yen 39,960,148,000. Adding the 1943-44 figure of
Yen 17,163,000,000, the grand total authorized up to
March 31, 1944, wi11 amount to Yen 57,123,148,000 against
total authorized expenditures of Yen 74,128,567,000.
An interesting departure from previous
methods of obtaining funds for military expenses is
found in the announcement that because the Greater
East Asia War is being carried on to decide the fate
of the races of Greater East Asia it is natural that
these races should, in accordance with their power,
bear the military expenses necessary for the prosecu-
tion of the war. Therefore it has been decided to
requisition for this purpose funds from various regions
of Greater East Asia and also call on the Southern
Development Bank for a loan. The amount of funds to
be so obtained is given at Yen 3,300,000,000.
It

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
5
It is also interesting to note the large
extent on which the Japanese Government has been depending
upon bond issues to finance war expenditures. The above
figures indicate that of Yen 74,128,567,000 of autho-
rized expenditures from July, 1937, to March 31, 1943,
Yen 57,123,148,000, or 77%, has been allocated to bond
issues.
SUBSIDIES
Only a few specific subsidy appropriations
are recorded in reports of the Diet meetings, but one
report gives an allotment of Yen 386,000,000 for sub-
sidies and encouragement funds for rice and other prin-
cipal foodstuffs for 1943-44. It was emphasized that
this amount is about twenty times that appropriated for
1937.
Another report is to the effect that the sum
of Yen 536,000,000 was incorporated in the budget as
subsidies for the three vital materials, steel, coal
and fuel. This figure is higher by Yen 83,000,000 than
that for 1942-43.It is assumed that this allotment
will be paid out as bonuses for production and for ex-
pansion to plants, mines, refineries, etc.

The Minister of Finance reportedly informed
the Diet on January 28, 1943, that the following im-
portant new expenditures are included in the General
Accounts budget for 1943-44:-

It is interesting to note that the Japanese
Government provided the sum of Yen 2,430,000 for ex-
penditures during 1942 and Yen 1,000,000 for 1943 to
"Rescue Japanese people who are interned in Hawaii,

NATIONAL INCOME - 1943-44
The Japanese Minister of Finance reportedly
informed the 8lst session of the Diet that the national
income of Japan for the year 1943-44 is estimated at
Yen 50,000,000,000. This amount represents an increase
of Yon 5,000,000,000 over the estimate for 1942, which
included incomes of Japanese individuals and corpora-
tions residing outside the Japanese Empire. The appor-
tionment of the 1943-44 income 1s given as follows:-
for taxes and similar debts of the people Yen
10,000,000,000; bonds Yen 21,000,000,000; for increased
production Yen 6,000,000,000; for 1iving costs of the
people Yen 13,000,000,000. The last item is lower by
Yen 2,000,000,000 than the amount al1owed for 1942-43
and reflects more rigid economy in living conditions.
Budget commitments for this fiscal year
amount to Yen 39,340,000,000 or 78-1/2% of the estimated
national income. The ratio of budget appropriations to
estimated national income in the 1942-43 fiscal year was
72%。
INCREASED NATIONAL SAVINGS
The goal for the 1942-43 fiscal year was set
at Yen 23,000,000,000. The 1evel for 1943-44 has been
fixed at Yen 27,000,000,000. The Minister of Finance
reportedly announced in the Diet on January 28, 1943,
that savings during the calendar year 1942 showed an
increase of Yen 21,317,000,000 over 1941.
GENERAL COMMENTS
From the reports of Japan's budgetary appro-
priations for the 1943-44 fiscal year 1t appears that
the internal financial situation is being maintained on
a fairly sound basis. One of the most encouraging
features, from Japan's standpoint, is the unusually
high rate of bond absorption. This may be the result
of greater Government pressure, but nevertheless it indi-
cates that larger volumes of private funds are flowing
into the Government's war efforts.
Under the circumstances it is not believed that
the very substantial increases in both the General
Accounts and Extraordinary Military budgets are excessive
or presage any serious strain on the nation's financial
structure. Commodity and wage prices apparently are being
held at reasonable levels and ail reports indicate a
sufficient quantity of food available. No extreme diffi-
oulties are likely to be encountered in obtaining the
budgetary requirements as sharp inoreases are reported
in

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
7
in bank balances, industrial profits, capital invest-
ments, corporate bond issues, industrial production,
and, of course, taxes.
Reports also indicate that Japan has been
fairly successful in administering the occupied areas
to the end that large volumes of essential materials
have found their way into Japanese industrial production.
Apparently efforts towards the stabilization of the
economy of occupied areas have also proven moderately
successful, at least to the extent of enabling the
populace to produce the required materials.

Reference Chungking's underlying despatch
no. 943 of February 18, 1943 on the subjeot
"Chinose Editorial Comment on Japanese Finan-
oial Situation".
There is enclosed with the Embassy's
despatoh a translation of an editorial from
the February 13, 1943 issue of the Ta Kung Pao
ontitled "Japan's War Finance on Brink of
Collapse". This editorial states that Japan's
budget for 1943 1s ¥36,995,000,000, which is
fourteen and a half times that of 1936 and
82% of the national income, that as in past
years the major proportion of national revenue
depends upon loans and that the result is
inflation. Substantial amounts (¥7,200,000,000
since July 7, 1937) have been invested in
Manohuria and north China, regarded by the
Ta Kung Pao as still the center of Japan's
resource development. The editorial quotes
Premier Tojo as stating Japan intends to devote
only 20% of its war expenditures to the China
theater and warns that Japan must have aggress-
ive designs in connection with expenditure of
the remaining 80%. The editorial concludes,
"Only a concerted Allied attack from many
directions can push Japan right down to the
abyss of financial collapse which may even
precede her military defeat."

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 1 to despatch no. 943 dated February 18,
1943 from American Embassy, Chungking, China.
( C O P Y )
JAPAN'S WAR FINANCE ON BRINK OF COLLAPSE
If a multi-front counter-offensive can be directed
against Japan by the United Nations now, her war finance
will race with her military factors on the road to ruin.
Holding this opinion, the Ta Kung Pao, on February 12,
urged the Allias to make a concerted attack on Japan while
her home finances are shaking.
Both from the angle of Janan's poverty and geographi-
cal size and the history of her finance, the present
¥27,000,0C0,000 emergency war budget for 1943 is indeed
startling news, the paper declared. Adding this to the
ordinary budget, the total national budget for Japan this
year is ¥36,995,000,000, which is 14 1/2 times bigger than
Japan's budget in 1936. The national incomes of the
Japanese people, according to the Japanese official announce-
ment, was V45,000,000,000. 1f this figure remains unchanged
for this year, then 82 per cent of the people's incomes in
1943 will be spent on the official ledger. This represents
not a small amount of the Jananese Government's seizure of
the people's earnings through toil and sweat.
While this new budget is already more than Japan can
bear, it is still insignificant if weighed on the scale of
the international budgetary race today. It is pitifully
small if compared with President Roosevelt's budget bill
of 1943 for the United States amounting to U.S.$100,000,-
000,000 which wil1 be ¥430,000,000,000 if converted into
Jananese money. In other words, America's budget is
eleven times as big as Janan's. Although Janan has boasted
of her sudden riches out of the present war, she cannot
keep from being "frugal" in making her budget. This,
according to the journal, symbolizes a painful contradiction
in Japan's national budget this year.
Compared with the 1942 emergency war budget of
¥18,000,000,000 which was passed in the 79th Diet Session,
it seems that this year's $27,000,C00,000 represents a
big increase. However, this is not the case. Japan used
in the first year after the outbreak of the Pacific war
an additiona1 ¥3,500,000,000 and ¥2,800,000,000, in the
77th and 78th emergency Diet sessions. Therefore, Japan's
actual total expenses in 1942 must have been ¥24,600,000,000.
This year's increase in the budget is only v2,400,000,000.
Japan has since last year included the expenditures
of the Ministry of War and Ministry of Navy in the emergency
war budget. The total war expenses of Japan occupied 75
per cent of the total budget in 1938. The retio war de-
croases to 72 the next year, to 71 in 1940 and to 67 in
1941. It was increased to 74 last year with the outbreak
of the Pacific War. The percentage for 1943 is 73. This
shows

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
shows that although the numerical figures of Japan's war
expenditures made geins year after year is the war, the
percentages in relation to the general budget have not
registered similar increases. In other words, although
Japan claims to be well-off financially, she cannot be
"generous" even in her war exnenditures. Further,
although Japan is belligerent, she cannot afford to arm
her troops to the teeth.
The Pacific War has increased Japan's war burden.
Her war expenditures increased threefold last year and
three and a half times this year. In 194l, Japan's war
expenditures totalled only 7,500,c00,C00. War expenditures
this year will equal 27 times the aggregate amount of army
and navy budgets in 1936, or seven times the figure in the
first year of her wer on China.
This rate of increase has shattered tht in the First
World War. Comparing the 1918 and 1914 war expenditures,
Great Britain registered a 12 times increase, France 9.7
times, and Germany .5 times. Now Janan's increase is
already 14 times over the year before the war. Since
Lukouchiao, Janan has spent V64,1C0,000,000 on her war
expenditures. By including her non-military expenses, the
grand total will be ¥107,300,000,C00, which, if v10 notes
are placed end to end together, will encircle the equator
40 times, according to the paper. Although Janan is stingy
and poor, war proves to be an expensive game just the same.
Taxation represents v6,500,000,00 in Japan's 1943
budget, showing an increase of 822,000,0~0 ver last year.
The plan for increasing taxation by ¥1,140,000,000 has
not yet been carried out, nor has the one for boosting
government profit ¥440,000,000 from tobacco monopoly.
In 1942, increases in direct taxation returns were more
than ¥1,200,000,000 and in indirect taxation returns more
than v700,000,000. The two tota1led v2,000,000,000, which
is double this year's possible increases. This shows that
Japan's national revenue can no longer place much hope on
taxation. In the last few years Janan's taxation usually
occupied 20 ner cent of the total national incone. In 1941,
the percentage increased to as much as 31 per cent. This
year, however, it will drop to 18. Although the percentage
has dropped, the burden of the Jananese people numerically
has increased.
The major portion of Jaran's national revenue still
depends on her loans. The ¥16,400,000,000 loans issued
2
last year took 64 per cent of her total national income.
The percentage of loans in Japan's war budget was 75 in
1938, 62 in 1939, 60 1n 1940, 54 in 1941. Since 1942,
the nercentage has again been on the upgrade. According
to statistics released last month, Janan's present
domestic indobtedness totalled ¥51,957,000,000, her
foreign indebtedness ¥1,221,000,000, making a grand total
of 53,178,000,000. If the late finance minister, Korekiyo
Takahashi's words thet $10,000,000,C00 of national debt
will mark Japan's doom can be taken for truth, Japan has
been doomed five times already. Before the end of this year,
she will be doomed seven times.
Inflation

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
Inflation is the only way out for Japan's increasing
loans. In July, 1937, the total amount of Japanese notes
in circulation was ¥1,640,000,000. At the end of 1942,
the total, including the Bank of Formosa and Bank of
Korea notes, must have exceeded ¥10,000,000,000 although
exact amounts are not available. Another way of absorb-
ing Japan's government bonds is savings. The attempt to
promote ¥23,000,000,000 of savings 1ast year was dis-
appointing. In addition to paying off taxes and getting
rid of government bonds, savings amounts have also been
used to extend productive enterprises which were given
¥6,000,000,000 1ast year. 0f this amount $2,100,000,000
were invested in Manchuria and China. Since July 7, 1937,
Japanese investments in Manchuria and China, according to
the journal, totalled ¥7,200,000,000. It shows that
Janan's development center of resources still lies in China's
Northeast and North China although she possesses the riches
of the South Seas. With the exception of oil, Manchuria
and North China have abundant resources such as iron and
coal which are lacking in the South Seas.
In conclusion, the Ta Kung Pao warns that judging by
Japan's huge war expenditures this year, something may be
afoot in the way of her future moves. Janan's emergency
war expenses in 1943 do not look like a defensive budget.
At least they show relentless efforts in making defensive
nreparations. Since Premier Tojo had said that Japan wants
only 20 per cent of her war expenditures on the China
field, the enormous amount of t27,000,000,000 for Janan
this year may indicate something sinister in Janan's mind.
The journal believes that Japan will not spend loo per cent
of her war expenditures or 82 per cent of her people's
total incomes for purely defensive purposes. Only a
concerted Allied attack from many directions can push
Japan fight down to the abyss of financial collapse which
may even precede her military defeat.
(Cori::

I have the honor to report that the February 5, 1943,
issue of El Nichia Djidji, one of the three Japanese lang-
894.
uage newspapers published in Buenos Aires, carried an item
which escaped notice at the time to the effect that the
Japanese national income for 1942 was roughly Yen 45 bil-
lions, or roughly US$lo billions, of which Finance Minister
Gaya announced that roughly two-thirds would be collected
in taxes by the national treasury. He also stated that it
was estimated that the Japanese national income for 1943
would be Yen 50 billions, or roughly US$ll billions, of
which it is probable that over two-thirds will be collected
5
by the treasury mainly for war use, and the living expenses
of the people will suffer a further reduction. If these
794.
figures are to be accepted it would appear that Japan spent
about US6.6 billions on the war in 1942, and plans to spend
about US$7.5 billions in 1943.
A translation of the article follows:
"Two-thirds of national income to be collected. Living stand-
ards to be further lowered. Tokyo 4th (Domei) "On February
3rd in the Diet Representative Nakamura at a general budget
meeting of the Lower House asked 'How about the national in-
come?' Finance Minister Gaya replied 'It is roughly esti-
mated that national income in 1943 will be Yen 50 billions'.
This represents an increase of 5 billions over the l942 na-
tional income of Yen 45 billions. Then the Finance Minister
added: 'For 1942, two-thirds of the national income is being
collected for the use of the country. For 1943 it is not un-
likely that that percentage will be further inoreased.' It

SECOND SUPPLEMENTARY BUDGET 1943-1944
ADOPTED IN JUNE 1943 BY
THE 82nd SEBSTON OF THE JAPANESE DIET
In the 82nd session of the Imperial Japanese Diet,
which was held from June 14 to June 17, 1943, a second
supplementary budget totaling 620,002,000 yen was adopted.
This sum, when added to the amounts appropriated under
various items in February 1943 at the previous session
of the Diet, brings the total amount appropriated for
the fiscal year April 1, 1943-March 30, 1944 to the grand
total of almost 40 billion yen. This exceeds by about
12 1/2 billion yen the sum appropriated for the previous
894.51/801
fiscal year, and which was in itself a record. The fol-
lowing table gives a breakdown of the 1943-44 budget:
Japan's Budget 1943-44
(in Yen 1,000)
Regular General Account
9,995,000
894.51
1st Supplementary (February 1943)
2,345,000
2nd
(June 1943)
620.004
Total General Account
12,960,004
Extraordinary Military Expenditure 27,000,000
Grand Total Budget
39,960,000
Previous Year Total
27,303,737
Japanese broadcasts have not seen fit to give any
PS/MB
revealing breakdown of the recent supplementary budget.
The most useful broadcast was monitored by New Delhi,
and it contained the following details:
Home Ministry
4,416,000ye
Finance Ministry
3,190,000
Agriculture Ministry
491,356,000
Commerce and Industry
63,479,000
Welfare Ministry
57,561.000
620,002,000
Little has been learned of the exaot purposes of
these expenditures, except that in one broadcast it was
stated that the 49i million yen allocated to the Ministry
of Agriculture and Forestry was ohiefly to be used for
rice subsidies. This presumably means that the subsidy
granted

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
granted the farmers for rice production has been increased.
In other broadoasts it has been noted that the Government
plans to stimulate the production of other cereals and of
potatoes.
The same broadoast monitored by New Delhi mentioned
above contained an unexplained list of the special accounts
budget, as tabulated below:
Offioe of Governor General of
Korea
140,414,000 yen
Food control in Korea
368,750,000
Office of Governor General of
Formosa
43,149,000
Rice control in Formosa
16,640,000
National Bond Adjustment Fund
420,874,000
National Bond Fund
440,008,000
Imperial Railways
2,000,000
68
These special accounts have never been included in the
main general accounts budget, and as there is little in-
dication of the use to which the funds will be put, comment
S
seems pointless. It is pointed out, however, that two
items are for "food control" in Korea and in Formosa.
Although specific statements in regard to the budget
were not monitored, the general impression was gained
that in addition to large grants of money for the purpose
of stimulating food production, the Government intended
in the 82nd session of the Diet to obtain funds necessary
for the implementation of the legislation for increased
control of industry which was passed at the same session.
For purposes of comparison, see memorandum dated
February 9, 1943, on 81st session of Diet, file 894.51
(copy in FE).

My dear Mr. Michael:
Referenoe is made to your letter of March 16, 1944
in which you state that one of your clients wishes to
file a claim with respect to certain bonds issued and
guaranteed by the Japanese Government.
The Department does not ordinarily intereede with
foreign governments on behalf of Amerioan nationals who
have purehased the bonds of such governments, It is to
be noted, moreover, that you do not state whether the
bends have matured or that there is any indication that
thore will be a default upon maturity. Purthermore, it
is met possible under existing conditions to take up
with enemy countries olaims which may be found to be
valid mor can it be foreseen when such a course may be-
come possible. Nevertheless, if your client wishes to
submit a statement to the Department concerning his bonds,
sueh a statement might follow the general lines suggested
by the enolosed memorandum prepared for fmerican owners
of property in enemy or enemy-occupied territory. Infor-
mation should be given regarding the identification and
deseription of the bonds, their face value and their pur-
chase price, the facts concerning your client's acquisition
of them, the last payment of interest made thereon, and
how and when your olient acquired Amerioan oitisenship.
Mo special forms are required.
Sincerely yours,

JAPANESE SPECIAL BUDGET BSTIMATES
FOR THE CONDUCT OP THE WAR.
(Translation from Berlin DEUTSCHER REICHSANZEIGER,
Jamuary :31, 1944)
.Tokyo, January 29, 1944. The Japanese Government
has presented to Parliament the special budget estimates
for the conduct of the war amounting to 38 billion yen
to be covered as follows: 2o.8 billion yen by Government
loans, 7 billion yen by the Overseas Davelopment Bureau
and the remainder from other souroes. ASAHI SHDBUM
reports that last year the Overseas Bureau contributed
to the financing of the war, by issuing bank-notes of the
Overseas Development Bureau amounting to 3.3 billion yen.
During the financial year 1944-45, the issue of Overseas
Bureau bank-notes will be inoreased to 7 billion yen.
After the elimination of overlapping items of the
national budget estimates and the special war budget
68
estimates, the total expenditures, according to AsAHI
SHMMBUM, will amount to 51 billion yen, compared to
37 billion yen in the preceding year. The looal news-
H
papers compare the loan requirements in the coming
fisoal year to that of the present year as follows:
5
loans for the national budget 6.l billion yen oompared to
3.5 billion yen in the present year, loans for the speoidl
war budget 2o.8 billion yen compared to 17.2 billiom yem,
and loans for other special budget items l.6 billion yen
806
compared to 1.2 billion yen, or a total of 28.5 billion
yen compared to 21.9 billion yen in the present year.

JAPAMBBE CIRCULATION OF PAPER CURRBBCY.
(Translation from BERLINER BOERSEN ZEITUNG, Pebruary 14,
1944.)
The ToYo KEIZAI writes regarding the inorease in the
ciroulation of paper ourrency at the end of l943 the
following: the issue of paper ourrenoy by the bank of
Japan at the end of 1943 amounted to lo.5 bill1on yen
oompared to 7.4 billion yen at the end of 1942. The 1ssue
of paper ourrency of the Mational Bank of Korea amoumted to l.46
billion yen at the end of 1943 compared to o.92 billion yen
the year before and the issue of paper currenoy of the Wational
Bank of Pormosa to o.41 billion yen at the end of 1943 oompared
to o.29 billion yen the year before. The Pinance Minister
gives three reasons for this increase:
1 - The inoreased needs of fueds by oompanies.
2 - The inorease in cash transactions and in cash held.
3 - The general inorease in finanoial and business
transactions.
The Pinance Minister stated in conneotion with the third
reason, that the present large oiroulation of paper currenay
was due to this tendenoy and was not an unhealthy sig. All
three reasons, however, are interdependent. The third reasen
reflects the development of industry and the inorease of war
production, so that the inorease in the ciroulation of paper
ourrenoy is a result of intensified war production and should
be melcomed. He also stated that because of the development
of industry, there wmas also an inoreame in the mumber of

sharply.

JAPANESE SAVINGS.
(Translat1on from Bssen NATIONAL ZEITUNG, February 21/22,1944)
(Tokyo) The Japanese Pinance Minister spoke at a Press
conference regarding savings in the fiscal year 1944-45. He
estimates the national income, as a result of the greatly
inoreased employment, will increase by lo billion yen to 6o
billion yen, of this income 36 billion yen will be saved
against 27 billion yen the previous year. Pourteen billion
yen will be needed for taxes so that lo billion yen will
remain for use by the people. The Government requirements
are estimated at 42.5 billion yen and 6 billion yen for
industrial plants, or a total of 48.6 billion yen. If 14
billion yen are covered by taxes, it would be necessary to save
34.6 b1111on yen.

1noreased employment, will inorease by lo billion yen to 6o

JAPANESE SPECIAL BUDGET ESTIMATES
FOR THE CONDUCT OF THE WAR.
(Translation from Berlin DEUTSCHER REICHSANZEIGER,
January :31, 1944)
Tokyo, January 29, 1944. The Japanese Government
has presented to Parliament the special budget estimates
for the conduct of the war amounting to 38 billion yen
to be covered as follows: 2o.8 billion yen by Government
loans, 7 billion yen by the Overseas Development Bureau
and the remainder from other sources. ASAHI SHIMBUM
reports that last year the Overseas Bureau contributed
to the financing of the war, by issuing bank-notes of the
Overseas Development Bureau amounting to 3.3 billion yen.
During the financial year 1944-45, the issue of Overseas
894.
Bureau bank-notes will be increased to 7 billion yen.
After the elimination of overlapping items of the
national budget estimates and the special war budget
S
estimates, the total expenditures, according to ASAHI
SHIMBUM, will amount to 51 billion yen, compared to
37 billion yen in the preceding year. The local news-
papers compare the loan requirements in the coming
fiscal year to that of the present year as follows:
loans for the national budget 6.l billion yen compared to
6
3.5 billion yen in the present year, loans for the special
war budget 2o.8 billion yen compared to 17.2 billion yen,
and loans for other special budget items l.6 billion yen
compared to 1.2 billion yen, or a total of 28.5 billion
yen compared to 2l.9 billion yen in the present year.

Losure No. 2 to despatch No. 7348
March 1, 1944, American Legation,Bern)
JAPANESE CIRCULATION OF PAPER CURRENCY.
(Translation from BERLINER BOERSEN ZEITUNG, February 14,
1944。)
The TOYO KEIZAI writes regarding the increase in the
circulation of paper currency at the end of 1943 the
following: the issue of paper currency by the bank of
Japan at the end of 1943 amounted to lo.5 billion yen
compared to 7.4 billion yen at the end of 1942. The issue
of paper currenoy of the National Bank of Korea amounted to 1.46
billion yen at the end of 1943 compared to o.92 billion yen
the year before and the issue of paper currenoy of the National
Bank of Formosa to o.41 billion yen at the end of 1943 compared
to o.29 billion yen the year before. The Finance Minister
gives three reasons for this increase:
1 - The increased needs of funds by companies.
2 - The increase in cash transactions and in cash held.
3 - The general increase in financial and business
transactions.
The Finence Minister stated in connection with the third
reason, that the present large circulation of paper currency
was due to this tendenoy and was not an unhealthy sign. All
three reasons, however, are interdependent. The third reason
reflects the development of industry and the increase of war
production, so that the increase in the circulation of paper
ourrency is a result of intensified war production and should
be welcomed. He also stated that because of the development
of industry, there was also an increase in the mumber of
workmen and a raising of wages. The requirements for money
to finance payments for forced labor and women workers increased
sharply.

FURTHER ADAPTATION OF JAPANESE
BUSINESS TO TOTAL WAR.
(Translation of article in "Die Deutsohe Volkswirt-
schaft", third issue of Deoember 1943.)
Yokohama, Middle December 1943.
Japan realises that the concentration of the Anglo-Amerioan
war effort in the Buropean war sone gives Japan a respite. This
is being fully used by Japan in order to prepare even more than
heretofore for the coming battles against its enemies. Every
day is used for the material and administrative expansion of the
armament of the greater Asiatio area, which is primarily dependent
on the efforts made in Japan. For this reason an economic
organisation is being oreated in Bastern Asia, with its center
in Japan, based on the armament industry. The ohanges in many
fields of busimess, administration and finance are evident.
169
Japanese Budget.

proposed Japanese budget for the fisoal year beginning in

SUBJECT:
JAPANESE DOLLAR BONDS.
DEPARTMENI OF STATE
From Mr. Lauchheim of S.E.C., I obtained the follow-
ing information on Japanese dollar bonds:
Shortly after Pearl Harbor the stock exchanges, at
the suggestion of SEc, delisted Japanese bonds along with
German and Italian bonds. The SEC also contacted the "over-
the-counter dealers" and requested them not to trade in
00
394551/5-2744
Japanese dollar bonds. Mr. Lauchheim bel ieves that there
is now no trading in these bonds and had no information as
to prices which might prevail for individual transaction
not handled through any organized securities market.
Mr. Lauchheim confirmed your belief that the Treasury
has permitted Japanese dollar bonds to be written off as
worthless by their holders for income tax purposes.
I decided that Mr. Lauchheim might be able to give
some helpful advice on the general subject of reparation
so I explained the background for the proposal.  He agreed
that an attempt by Treasury to buy up the bonds at this
time would likely have serious domestic repercussions,
particularly since the restrictions placed upon trading
might be said to have in themselves reduced the value of
the bonds. He stated that in his opinion if the Treasury
were to offer to buy at say lo on the dollar, a speculative
CS
market would probably develop at 12 or 13 on the dollar
and that in order to obtain any substantial number of bonds
s/W
the Treasury would have to 1ssue a definite order prohibit-
ing the sale of bonds to any buyer except Treasury. This
Government would then be in the position of having fixed
the value of the bonds and trying to persuade their holders
to sell. If at a later time it should turn out that the
reparation settlements with various enemy nations provide
for payment of pre-war obligations at par, those who had
sold Japanese bonds to the Treasury at lo on the dollar
would feel aggrieved. There is, of course, the further
894.51/5.27
D
point that any offer for Japanese bonds would lead holders
4
of German, Polish, or Russian bonds et al to request similar
treatment.
Cat M

the Securities and Exchange Commission would do every-
thing that it could to facilitate the operation in case
Treasury and State Departments should decide to undertake
1t.

Mr. Ballantine
Supplementary to my memorandum of July 25, 1941,
which pertained to efforts on the part of the Yokahama
Specie Bank of San Francisco to send $5,000,000 of
Government bonds out of the country, I submit the fol-
894.51/8-241
lowing information which was communicated to me by
Mr. Spear of the F.B.I.
The efforts of a special bank examiner to locate
the $5,000,000 in bonds as previously reported were
unsuccessful. The morning of July 26, 194l (the day of
the issuance of the President's order freezing the funds
of the Japanese) a Japanese messenger arrived at the
bank. Through careful questioning it was learned that
the $5,000,000 in bonds were concealed in the basement
of the bank. An in vestigation looated these bonds, but
1nstead of the $5,000,000 reported, there were actually
bonds to the value of $5,554,900. These were Japanese
CS/
Government bonds, but-the-exact issue is as yet unknown.
DC/R

Anal
8

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Further examination will reveal the exact issuance. It
is believed that these bonds were to have been transported
in secrecy from the bank to the Japanese ship, Tatuta Haru,
where they were to have been placed in a special tank which
had been arranged for this purpose.
On July 29, 1941, a shipment of Japanese Government
bonds valued at $1,737,000 arrived at San Francisco from
the Yokahama Specie Bank in New York. It is reported that
this last shipment has been impounded. The Yokahama Specie
Bank at San Francisoo maintains that the $5,554,900 in
bonds had been officially canceled upon arrival in San
Francisco and that these bonds were worthless. This state-
ment is being carefully checked by the bank examiner.
The total of Japanese Government bonds amounts to
7,290,900.
